### [QROC] Explain: Glutathione Peroxidase as an Antioxidant:
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Glutathione peroxidase is an antioxidant enzyme that detoxifies hydrogen peroxide and lipid peroxides.
It uses reduced glutathione (GSH), converting it into oxidized glutathione (GSSG).
GSSG is then recycled back to GSH by glutathione reductase using NADPH.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Benefits of Free Radicals?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. NADPH-Oxidase & Myeloperoxidase.
2. NO is a vasodilator, second messenger, neurotransmitter, lethal to microorganisms.
3. Mediates action of some chemotherapeutics, killing cancer cells.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Oxidative Stress (definition & causes)?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Def: imbalance between Pro-oxidants and antioxidants.
causes: Increase formation of FREE RADICALS, Increase availability of transition metal ions, Decreased body antioxidant system.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Free radicals (definition & Clinical consequences)?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Def: an atom or molecule containing one or more unpaired electrons in its outer orbit.
Clinical: 1.Chronic Inflammation, 2.Cardiovascular diseases (CHD), 3. Cancer, 4. Cataract, 5.Respiratory diseases, 6.Diabetes, 7.Male infertility.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Define antioxidants?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Substances, that protect us against harmful effects of free radicals.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate types of antioxidants (According to nature) & (according to mode of action)?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1- According to nature:
a. Antioxidants enzymes b. Nutritional antioxidants c. Soluble antioxidants d. Transition metals Chelator
2-According to mode of action
a. Preventive antioxidants as catalase
b. Chain breaking antioxidants: SOD

### [QROC] Define of Rancidity?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Rancidity is the spoilage of fats and oils resulting in unpleasant odor and taste due to oxidation or hydrolysis of lipids.

*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the types of Rancidity?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: a. Oxidative rancidity
b. Hydrolytic rancidity
C. Ketonic rancidity

### [QROC] Explain oxidative rancidity?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: a. Addition of antioxidants (e.g. Vitamin E, Vitamin C, BHA, BHT)
b. Proper storage away from air, light, and heat
C. Refrigeration
D. Use of airtight containers
E. Removal of oxygen (vacuum packing)

### [QROC] What are the important characteristics of reactive oxygen species (ROS)?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Extreme reactivity, short life span, generation of new ROS by chain reaction, and damage to various tissues.

*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

### [QROC] Clinical Significance of essential fatty acids (PUFA)?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. cannot be synthesized by the body and have to be supplied in the diet
2. Linoleic and Linolenic acids cannot be synthesized by the body.
3. Polyunsaturated fatty acids are also designated as in:
ω3 (omega 3) family: Linolenic acids.
ω6 (omega 6) family: Linoleic and Arachidonic acids.
4. The pentaenoic acid present in fish oils is of great nutritional importance (ω3) unsaturated fatty acid.

*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Define: Zwitter ion?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: An amino acid carrying both positive and negative charges at physiological pH, making it electrically neutral and unable to move in an electric field.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Define Essential amino acids and list two examples.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Amino acids that cannot be synthesized by the body and must be obtained from the diet.
Examples: Valine, Leucine, Threonine, Lysine, Phenylalanine.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Define Isoelectric point (IEP)?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: The pH at which an amino acid exists as a zwitter ion. Example: IEP of alanine = 6.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Define ketogenic amino acids and list two examples?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Amino acids whose metabolism produces ketone bodies. Examples: Lysine and Leucine.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Define peptide bond?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: A covalent bond formed between the α-carboxyl group of one amino acid and the α-amino group of another with the loss of a water molecule, forming peptides and proteins.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Diseases related to misfolding of proteins?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Alzheimer's disease and Parkinson's disease.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Apoenzyme?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: is inactive enzyme without its non protein moiety(cofactor).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Holoenzyme?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: is an active enzyme with its non protein component(cofactor).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Cofactor?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: is a non-protein chemical compound that is bound (either tightly or loosely) to an enzyme and is required for its activity.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Compare Coenzyme and Prosthetic Groups
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Prosthetic groups: Inorganic, Thermo-stable, Tightly bound to apoenzyme, Metals (Zn2+, Fe2+)
- Coenzyme: Organic, Thermo-labile, Loosely bound to apoenzyme, Vit B derivatives (NAD, FAD)
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enzymes?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Bio catalysts that regulate the rate at which all biochemical reactions take place.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Active Site?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: A specific region on the enzyme molecule where the substrate binds and the reaction occurs.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Define Zymogens with example?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Zymogens (proenzymes) are inactive precursors of enzymes that require specific biochemical cleavage of one or more peptide bonds to convert into their catalytically active forms. Example: Pepsinogen (cleaved by HCl into active pepsin) or Trypsinogen (cleaved by enterokinase into active trypsin).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026, Exams, Final 2025*

---

### [QROC] Energy of Activation
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: All chemical reactions require a specific amount of energy to start.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Mechanism of Action
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: The enzyme (E) binds with the substrate (S) to form an Enzyme-Substrate complex (ES), which then converts into Product (P) and releases the free enzyme.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Lock and Key Model?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: The active site has a fixed, rigid shape that exactly matches the substrate.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Induced Fit Model?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: The active site is flexible and changes its shape to fit the substrate more closely upon binding.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Mention three functions of glycoprotein?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. They act as structural components of cell membranes.
2. They play an important role in cell recognition and cell adhesion.
3. They are involved in immune functions.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate three clinical importances of hyaluronic acid?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Treatment of osteoarthritis (intra-articular injection as a synovial fluid supplement / viscosupplementation).
2. Ophthalmic surgery (used as a viscoelastic agent during cataract and corneal transplantation surgery).
3. Wound healing and skin care / plastic surgery (tissue regeneration, hydration, and cosmetic dermal fillers).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026, Exams, Final 2025*

---

### [QROC] Define of Zwitter ion?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: An amino acid or dipolar molecule that carries both a positive charge (-NH3+) and a negative charge (-COO-) at physiological pH (isoelectric point), resulting in a net electrical charge of zero and inability to migrate in an electric field.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026, Exams, Final 2025*

---

### [QROC] Explain why changes in pH can affect the catalytic activity of the enzyme?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Changes in pH alter the ionization (charge) state of the acidic and basic amino acid side chains at the enzyme's active site and on the substrate. Extreme pH changes can disrupt ionic and hydrogen bonds maintaining the tertiary structure, causing denaturation and loss of catalytic function. Each enzyme operates at an optimum pH.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026, Exams, Final 2025*

---

### [QROC] Biochemical basis of Respiratory Distress Syndrome (RDS)?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Neonatal respiratory distress syndrome (hyaline membrane disease) is caused by a deficiency of lung surfactant (primarily dipalmitoylphosphatidylcholine / lecithin), synthesized by Type II alveolar cells. Deficiency causes high alveolar surface tension, leading to alveolar collapse (atelectasis) during expiration and severe hypoxemia.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Biochemistry 2026, Exams, Final 2025*

---

### [QROC] Describe the structure of the cell membrane and explain the significance of its lipid bilayer?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Structure:** It is approximately 7.5 nm thick. In Electron Microscopy (EM), it appears trilaminar (unit membrane) with two electron-dense layers separated by an electron-lucent zone.
* **Significance:** The lipid bilayer is formed mainly of phospholipids and cholesterol. The hydrophilic heads face the surface, while the hydrophobic tails face each other in the interior. This structure maintains the structural integrity and creates a semi-permeable barrier.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain the roles of proteins in the cell membrane. Differentiate between intrinsic and extrinsic proteins?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Roles:** Proteins act as ion channels, carrier proteins for transport, and cell receptors for drugs and hormones.
* **Intrinsic (Integral) Proteins:** Embedded within the membrane thickness; some span the entire thickness (transmembrane proteins).
* **Extrinsic (Peripheral) Proteins:** Located on the cytoplasmic or extracellular surfaces and do not enter the lipid bilayer.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] What is the glycocalyx, and what functions does it serve in the cell membrane?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Definition:** A carbohydrate layer (glycoproteins and glycolipids) on the external surface of the plasma membrane.
* **Functions:** * Cell-to-cell adhesion.
* Contains antigens like Major Histocompatibility Complexes (MHC).
* Repels adjoining cells due to its negative charge.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Discuss the structural differences between Rough and Smooth Endoplasmic Reticulum and their respective functions?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Rough ER (RER):** Consists of parallel flattened sacs (cisternae) studded with ribosomes. Function: Protein synthesis (secretory, lysosomal, and membrane proteins).
* **Smooth ER (SER):** System of interconnecting tubules without ribosomes. Function: Synthesis of steroid hormones, lipids, and glycogen; detoxification; and calcium storage in muscles.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] What is the structural significance of the double membrane structure of mitochondria?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * The outer membrane contains porins for permeability. The inner membrane forms **cristae** to increase surface area for respiratory enzymes. It is rich in cardiolipin, making it impermeable to ions.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Compare and contrast lysosomes and peroxisomes in terms of structure and function?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Lysosomes:** Contain hydrolytic enzymes (pH 5.0) for intracellular digestion. Formed from the Golgi complex.
* **Peroxisomes:** Contain oxidative enzymes (peroxidase, catalase) for fatty acid oxidation and neutralizing free radicals. They are self-replicating and not formed in Golgi.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the four main components of the cytoplasm?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Cytoplasmic matrix (cytosol / cell sap).
2. Cytoplasmic organelles (membranous and non-membranous).
3. Cytoskeleton (microtubules, microfilaments, intermediate filaments).
4. Cytoplasmic inclusions (stored foods, pigments, crystals).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026, Exams, Final 2025*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the membranous organelles?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Cell membrane
2. Mitochondria
3. Golgi apparatus
4. Lysosomes
5. Endoplasmic reticulum (RER & SER)
6. Peroxisomes
7. Endosomes
8. Coated vesicles.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the non-membranous organelles?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Ribosomes
2. Centrioles
3. Microtubules
4. Microfilaments
5. Cilia
6. Flagella.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Write the Molecular structure of the cell membrane?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Lipids (30%):** Phospholipids and Cholesterol.
* **Proteins (60%):** Integral and Peripheral proteins.
* **Carbohydrates (10%):** Glycolipids and Glycoproteins (Glycocalyx).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Writ the E/M structure of Mitochondria?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * Enclosed by two membranes. The inner membrane folds into cristae. The matrix contains DNA, ribosomes, and elementary particles (ATP synthase).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Write the types of MitochondIria?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. **Orthodox type:** Well-defined cristae (inactive).
2. **Condensed type:** Ill-defined cristae (active).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Write the E/M structure of Golgi apparatus?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * Consists of several flattened saccules (cisternae) arranged in stacks. It has a **Cis-face** (convex/immature) and a **Trans-face** (concave/mature).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Write the E/M structure of Lysosome?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * Small membrane-bound dense bodies (0.2 to 0.55 um) containing over 50 acid hydrolases.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Write the E/M structure of Peroxisomes?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * Microbodies (200-500 nm) with a crystalline or dense appearance.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the types of Ribosomes?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Attached ribosomes (on RER).
2. Free ribosomes (in cytosol).
3. Polyribosomes (Polysomes).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Write the E/M structure of Centriole?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * Consists of nine triplets of fused microtubules (A, B, and C microtubules) arranged in a cylinder.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Write the types of Microfilaments?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Thick Filaments (Myosin).
2. Thin Filaments (Actin).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Write the types of intermediate filaments?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Keratin (epithelial cells).
2. Vimentin (fibroblasts).
3. Desmin (muscle cells).
4. Neurofilaments (nerve cells).
5. Glial filaments (glial cells).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the Apical Cell Surface specializations?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Apical:** Microvilli, Stereocilia, Cilia.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the Basal Cell Surface specializations?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Basement membrane (basal lamina and reticular lamina).
2. Basal plasma membrane infoldings (with aligned mitochondria for active ion/water transport).
3. Hemidesmosomes (attach basal epithelial cells to the underlying basement membrane).
4. Focal adhesions.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026, Exams, Final 2025*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the Lateral Cell Surface specializations?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Lateral:** Tight junctions, Anchoring junctions (Zonula/Macula adherens), and Gap junctions.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Write the E/M structure of the Nucleus?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * Composed of the nuclear envelope, chromatin, nucleolus, and nucleoplasm.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Write the E/M structure of the Nuclear Envelope?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * Two concentric unit membranes separated by perinuclear space. The outer membrane is continuous with RER. It contains nuclear pores closed by pore complexes.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] What are the differences between Cilia and microvilli?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Cilia:** Motile, contain a microtubule core (axoneme 9+2), and long.
* **Microvilli:** Non-motile, contain an actin filament core, and increase surface area for absorption.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain the role of the basement membrane in epithelial tissue?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Provides adhesion between epithelium and connective tissue.
2. Acts as a barrier to molecular diffusion.
3. Influences nerve regeneration and re-establishment of neuromuscular junctions.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] What are stereocilia, and where are they typically found?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * They are very long microvilli (15-20 um). Found in the inner ear hair cells, epididymis, and vas deferens.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Describe the histological characteristics of Cilia?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **LM:** Apical acidophilic striations.
* **EM:** Covered by cell membrane; core (axoneme) has 9 pairs of peripheral microtubules and 2 central singlets.
### Epithelium
**B. Answer the following questions.**
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerat the four basic tissues in the body?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Epithelial tissue.
2. Connective tissue.
3. Muscle tissue.
4. Nervous tissue.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Discribe the general character of epithelial tissue?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * Single or multiple layers of close-packed cells with very little extracellular material.
* Held together by intercellular junctions.
* Lying on the basement membrane.
* Avascular, nourished by diffusion from the underlying connective tissue layer.
* Rich in sensory nerve endings.
* Has the capacity to regenerate.
* Derived from all three primordial germ layers (ectoderm, mesoderm, and endoderm).
* Covers the body surface, lines cavities/organs, and constitutes most glands.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Classify the Epithelium?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Covering/Lining Epithelium.
2. Glandular Epithelium.
3. Special types (e.g., Neuro-epithelium and Myo-epithelium).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Classify the Covering/Lining Epithelium?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Number of cell layers:** Simple (one layer) and Stratified (multiple layers).
* **Shape of the cells:** Squamous, Cuboidal, and Columnar.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Write the types of Simple Epithelium?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Simple Squamous Epithelium.
2. Simple Cuboidal Epithelium.
3. Simple Columnar Epithelium.
4. Pseudostratified Columnar Epithelium.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Write the types of Stratified Epithelium?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Stratified Squamous Epithelium.
2. Stratified Cuboidal Epithelium.
3. Stratified Columnar Epithelium.
4. Transitional Epithelium.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Describe the Microscopic appearance of Simple Squamous Epithelium?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * Single layer of flattened cells.
* Nucleus is flattened in the plane of the cell.
* Cytoplasm is very thin and tough to see.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Describe the Microscopic appearance of Simple Cuboidal Epithelium?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * Single layer of cuboidal-shaped cells.
* Centrally-placed round nuclei.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Describe the Microscopic appearance of Simple Columnar Epithelium?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * Single layer of tall, narrow columnar cells.
* Basal, oval nuclei.
* Secretory vesicles may be visible in the apical portion.
* Microvilli and cilia may be present.
* Goblet cells are often interspersed.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Describe the Microscopic appearance of Pseudostratified Columnar Epithelium?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * Looks multi-layered, but is NOT (all cells touch the basement membrane).
* Cells are of varying heights, giving a stratified appearance.
* Nuclei are at several levels.
* Often has goblet cells and cilia.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Describe the Microscopic appearance of Stratified Squamous Epithelium?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * Multiple cell layers with flattening toward the surface.
* Two types: **Keratinized** (apical layers are dead cells without nuclei, packed with keratin) and **Non-keratinized** (lacks dead cell layers at the surface).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Describe the Microscopic appearance of Stratified Cuboidal Epithelium?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * Two or more layers of cells.
* Surface layers are cuboidal.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Describe the Microscopic appearance of Stratified Columnar Epithelium?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * Two or more layers of cells.
* Surface cells are tall columnar with basally located nuclei.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Describe the Microscopic appearance of Transitional Epithelium?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * Similar to stratified squamous, but surface cells are rounded and bulge (dome-shaped).
* Thickness varies: 5-6 layers when relaxed, 2-3 layers when stretched.
* Cells become flatter and thinner when stretched.
* Some cells are bi-nucleated.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Classify the exocrine gland according to type (nature) of secretion?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * Mucous (e.g., goblet cell).
* Serous (e.g., parotid gland).
* Mixed (e.g., mixed salivary gland).
* Fatty (e.g., sebaceous glands).
* Watery (e.g., sweat glands).
* Cellular (e.g., ovary and testes).
* Milky (e.g., mammary gland).
* Wax (e.g., glands in external ear).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Classify the exocrine gland according to according to mode (mechanism) of secretion?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Merocrine:** Release via exocytosis (most common).
* **Holocrine:** Release of whole cells or breakdown of the entire cell (e.g., sebum).
* **Apocrine:** A portion of the apical cytoplasm is lost with the secretion (e.g., mammary glands).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Classify the exocrine gland according to branching of the duct?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Simple:** Single non-branching duct.
* **Simple Branched:** Single non-branching duct with a branched secretory portion.
* **Compound:** Branching duct system.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Classify the exocrine gland according to shape of secretory part?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Tubular:** In the form of a tube.
* **Alveolar or Acinar:** Rounded secretory part.
* **Tubulo-Alveolar (acinar):** Flask-shaped secretory part.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain the differences between keratinized and non-keratinized stratified squamous epithelium?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Keratinized:** Apical layers are dead cells, lack nuclei, and are packed with keratin protein. Found in epidermis (palms/soles). Protects against abrasion, water loss, and pathogens.
* **Non-keratinized:** Lacks dead cell layers at the surface. Found in oral cavity, esophagus, vagina, and anal canal. Protects from mechanical abrasions during eating, swallowing, and defecation.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Describe the microscopic appearance of stratified columnar epithelium and its locations?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Appearance:** Two or more layers; surface cells are tall columnar with basal nuclei.
* **Locations:** Rare type. Non-ciliated found in parts of the anal canal, pharynx, and epiglottis; Ciliated found in fetal esophagus.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Analyze the protective functions of stratified squamous epithelium in various body locations?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Keratinized type (Skin):** Provides a barrier against mechanical abrasion, prevents water loss, and blocks entry of pathogens.
* **Non-keratinized type (Esophagus, Vagina, etc.):** Protects wet surfaces from mechanical friction and abrasion caused by food passage, sexual intercourse, childbirth, and defecation.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Discuss the classification of exocrine glands based on the mode of secretion?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Merocrine:** Release via exocytosis (most common).
* **Holocrine:** Release of whole cells or breakdown of the entire cell (e.g., sebum).
* **Apocrine:** A portion of the apical cytoplasm is lost with the secretion (e.g., mammary glands).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] What are the characteristics of simple and compound exocrine glands?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Simple Glands:** Possess a single, non-branching duct.
* **Compound Glands:** Possess a branching duct system.
### Connective tissue
**B. Answer the following questions.**
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Describe the histological appearance of macrophages under light microscopy?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Large, irregularly shaped cells with irregular surface projections (pseudopodia). Cytoplasm is basophilic, vacuolated, and contains ingested particles/phagolysosomes. Nucleus is small, dark, indented, kidney-shaped or eccentric (heterochromatic). Identified by vital staining (e.g., trypan blue) due to active phagocytosis.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026, Exams, Final 2025*

---

### [QROC] Explain the differences between collagen, reticular, and elastic fibers?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Collagen Fibers:** Most abundant, thick, wavy, non-branched, and white in color. They are very strong with high tensile strength.
* **Reticular Fibers:** Thinner than collagen, branched, and form delicate networks (reticulum). They are made of collagen type III and are argyrophilic (stained black with silver).
* **Elastic Fibers:** Thinner than collagen, straight, branched, and highly deformable (can stretch and recoil).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] What are glycosaminoglycans (GAGs), and what role do they play in connective tissue?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Definition:** Polysaccharides composed of repeating non-branching disaccharides.
* **Role:** They are highly negatively charged and hydrophilic, attracting sodium ions and water. This creates a hydrated, gel-like matrix that bears mechanical forces and regulates the passage of molecules.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Discuss the structural characteristics of elastic fibers and their functional significance?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Structure:** Formed of an elastin core surrounded by microfibrils of fibrillin-I and elastin.
* **Significance:** They provide elasticity to tissues, allowing them to stretch (up to 150%) and recoil. They are found in areas like the aorta and elastic arteries.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Describe the appearance of reticular fibers under light microscopy and their functional role in tissues?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Appearance:** Very thin fibers that branch and anastomose to form a delicate network. They are not stained by H&E but appear black with silver stain.
* **Role:** They form the supporting stroma for lymphoid organs, bone marrow, and internal organs like the liver and lungs.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Differentiate between dense regular and dense irregular collagenous connective tissue?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Dense Regular:** Collagen fibers are arranged regularly. Found in tendons and the cornea.
* **Dense Irregular:** Collagen fibers are randomly arranged. Found in ligaments and organ capsules.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Describe the vascularity of areolar connective tissue and its significance?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Vascularity:** It is highly vascular.
* **Significance:** It serves as a reservoir of water and salts. Almost all cells obtain nutrients and release wastes through it.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] What is the extracellular matrix, and what are its components in connective tissue?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Definition:** The non-cellular part of the tissue.
* **Components:** Fibers (collagen, elastic, reticular) and Ground Substance (water, salts, GAGs, and glycoproteins).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] How does the structure of dense connective tissue differ from loose connective tissue?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Dense CT:** High fiber content, low cell content, and low ground substance.
* **Loose (Areolar) CT:** Abundant ground substance, scattered cells, and loosely arranged fibers.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain how connective tissue contributes to the immune response?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * It contains transient immune cells like **plasma cells** (produce antibodies), **mast cells** (release inflammatory mediators), **macrophages** (phagocytosis and antigen presentation), and **leukocytes**.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain the structural characteristics of plasma cells and their significance?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: * **Structure:** Oval cell with basophilic cytoplasm, eccentric "cartwheel" or "clock-face" nucleus, and a negative Golgi image.
* **Significance:** Responsible for humoral immunity by producing antibodies (immunoglobulins).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Undifferentiated mesenchymal cells (UMCs).
2. Fibroblasts and fibrocytes.
3. Adipocytes (fat cells).
4. Reticular cells.
5. Pericytes.

*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the Free (Transient) CT cells?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Mast cells.
2. Plasma cells.
3. Macrophages.
4. WBCs (Leukocytes).
5. Pigment cells.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Histology 2026*

### [QCS] What is the best definition of homeostasis?

- **D)** The constant increase of physiological functions with time
- **E)** The maintenance of physiological variables within a relatively constant range

### [QCS] Which of the following is considered a "variable" in homeostasis?

- **B)** Height
- **C)** Temperature
- **D)** Genetic code
- **E)** Bone density

*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

### [QCS] Which of the following statements is TRUE about homeostasis?

- **B)** Homeostasis regulates only extracellular variables.
- **C)** Disease occurs when homeostatic correction is efficient.
- **D)** Correction mechanisms depend on the degree of deviation from the set-point.
- **E)** Homeostasis operates through voluntary control only.

*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QCS] Which of the following best describes the role of a sensor (receptor) in a homeostatic control system?

- **B)** Determines the normal range of variables
- **C)** Continuously monitors changes in the controlled variable
- **D)** Transmits impulses to muscles and glands for action
- **E)** Stores the set-point for long-term memory
**Correct Answer**: C
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QCS] What is the function of the comparator (control center) in homeostasis?

- **B)** Generates changes in the external environment
- **C)** Directly induces effector action
- **D)** Compares input from sensors to the set point to detect deviation
- **E)** Produces variable changes in body structure
**Correct Answer**: D
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

### [QCS] Which of the following statements best describes a negative feedback mechanism?

- **B)** It maintains variables at a fixed point only
- **C)** It amplifies the deviation from the set point
- **D)** It counteracts changes and restores balance
- **E)** It always leads to a harmful outcome
**Correct Answer**: D

### [QCS] Which of the following is an example of a positive feedback mechanism?

- **B)** Regulation of blood pressure
- **C)** Childbirth (parturition)
- **D)** Blood glucose control
- **E)** Water balance
**Correct Answer**: C
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QCS] What is the primary purpose of feedforward (adaptive) control?

- **C)** To prevent future changes in variable by anticipating them
- **D)** To shut down feedback loops
- **E)** To oppose positive feedback mechanisms
**Correct Answer**: C
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

### [QCS] Which of the following best illustrates the concept of negative feedback?

- **D)** Decreased glucose stimulates insulin release
- **E)** Pain causes increased heart rate

- **D)** Generation of nerve impulse

### [QCS] Which of the following statements is TRUE about homeostatic control systems?

**Correct Answer**: C
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

### [QROC] Enumerate the mechanisms that stimulate the thirst center centrally.

**Explanation**: 1. Increased osmolality of ECF (hypertonicity)
2. Decreased ECF or plasma volume
3. Decreased arterial blood pressure
4. Increased angiotensin II acting on subfornical organ and organum vasculosum
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate mechanisms that increase water loss when body water increases.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Pressure diuresis
2. Pressure natriuresis
3. Increased ANP secretion
4. Decreased ADH secretion
5. Decreased aldosterone secretion
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the sources of daily water gain.

**Explanation**: 1. Drinking fluids (1400 ml)
2. Water from food (700 ml)
3. Metabolic (oxidation) water (200 ml)
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Why is thirst activated when plasma osmolality increases?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Increased osmolality causes osmotic withdrawal of water from osmoreceptor neurons, leading to their shrinkage and activation of the thirst center in the hypothalamus.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Why does hypovolemia stimulate both thirst and ADH secretion?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Low blood volume reduces baroreceptor activity, stimulating the hypothalamus to increase ADH secretion and activate the thirst center to restore blood volume.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Why does inhibition of sympathetic activity lead to increased urine volume?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Reduced sympathetic tone causes renal vasodilation, increasing renal blood flow and GFR, leading to enhanced urine formation.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain the effect of increased ANP secretion on water balance?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: It promotes natriuresis and diuresis by increasing sodium and water excretion, reducing blood volume and pressure.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain the effect decreased plasma osmolality on water intake?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Thirst sensation is suppressed, leading to reduced water intake to prevent overhydration.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain the mechanism by which thirst is stimulated by hypertonicity.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Hypertonic ECF draws water out of osmoreceptor neurons → neurons shrink → thirst center in hypothalamus is activated → person feels thirsty → drinks water → osmolality normalizes.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain the mechanism of ADH release in response to hypovolemia.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Reduced blood volume decreases baroreceptor firing → hypothalamus senses this via afferent pathways → posterior pituitary releases ADH → water reabsorption increases in kidneys.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain how pressure diuresis helps restore water balance.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Increased blood pressure → ↑ renal perfusion → ↑ GFR → ↑ urine formation → reduction of excess body water and blood pressure.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain the mechanism of decreased water output during dehydration.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Low blood volume and pressure → ↑ renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system and ADH → ↑ water and Na+ reabsorption → ↓ urine output.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Describe how thirst is triggered by peripheral mechanisms.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Dry mouth and pharynx (due to reduced salivary secretion) → sensory input via glossopharyngeal and vagus nerves → CNS → thirst sensation → water intake initiated.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Match the hormone with its effect on water balance: | Hormone | Effect | |---|---| | A. ADH | 1. Water retention | | B. ANP | 2. Water and Na+ excretion | | C. Aldosterone | 3. Na+ reabsorption |
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Correct Matching: A → 1 B → 2 C → 3
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Correct Matching: A → 1 B → 2 C → 3
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

### [QROC] Enumerate the types of passive transport across cell membranes
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Simple diffusion
2. Facilitated diffusion
3. Osmosis
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the requirements for osmosis to occur.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. A solute concentration gradient across a semipermeable membrane
2. A membrane relatively impermeable to the solute (osmotically active solute)
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the types of protein channels in the plasma membrane.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: A. leak (non gated) channels
B. Gated channels: include
1. Voltage-gated channels
2. Ligand (chemical)-gated channels
3. Mechanically-gated channels
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the properties of facilitated diffusion.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Follows concentration gradient
2. No energy required
3. Requires specific carrier proteins
4. Subject to saturation
5. Shows specificity and competitive inhibition
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the factors that affect the rate of diffusion across membranes.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Concentration gradient
2. Electrical potential difference
3. Pressure gradient
4. Membrane surface area
5. Membrane permeability
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the characteristics of carrier-mediated transport.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Saturable at high substrate concentration
2. Structurally specific for substrates
3. Subject to competitive inhibition
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate characteristics of simple diffusion
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: a. Occurs with concentration gradient
b. Does not need energy
c. Does not need carrier
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Why is facilitated diffusion saturable at high solute concentrations?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Because the number of carrier proteins is limited. Once all carriers are occupied (saturated), increasing solute concentration does not increase transport rate further.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Why does glucose not diffuse freely through the lipid bilayer?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Glucose is a large, uncharged, polar molecule and not lipid-soluble, so it requires a specific carrier for facilitated diffusion.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Why does NaCl have greater osmotic activity than glucose?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: NaCl dissociates into two ions (Na+ and Cl-), contributing 2 osmoles per mole, while glucose remains undissociated and contributes only 1 osmole per mole.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Why is water able to diffuse through the lipid bilayer despite being polar?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Water is a very small molecule with high kinetic energy, allowing it to pass through the bilayer or protein channels (aquaporins).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Why does increasing the thickness of the membrane reduce the rate of diffusion?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Thicker membranes increase the distance a molecule must travel, thereby slowing down the rate of diffusion according to Fick's law.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Describe the mechanism of facilitated diffusion.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: The solute binds to a specific site on the carrier protein -> conformational change in the protein exposes the solute to the opposite side of the membrane -> solute is released.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain the mechanism by which voltage-gated ion channels operate.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: They open or close in response to changes in membrane potential, allowing selective ion passage (e.g., Na+, K+).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Describe how ligand-gated ion channels function.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: A specific chemical (e.g., neurotransmitter or hormone) binds to the channel protein -> causes a conformational change -> channel opens to allow ion flow.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain the mechanism behind cell shrinkage in hypertonic solution.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Hypertonic ECF has higher solute concentration -> water moves out of the cell via osmosis -> cell shrinks (crenation).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain the mechanism behind saturation of carrier-mediated transport.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: When all carriers are occupied by substrate, transport rate reaches a maximum (Vmax); further increase in substrate concentration does not increase transport rate.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] What is the effect of increasing the number of protein channels on diffusion rate?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: It increases the rate of diffusion for substances passing through those channels.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain the effect on water movement when a cell is placed in a hypotonic solution ?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Water moves into the cell, leading to swelling and potentially lysis.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] What is the effect of increasing lipid solubility of a substance on its diffusion?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Increases the rate of diffusion across the lipid bilayer.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] What is the effect of high molecular weight on the diffusion rate of a substance?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: It decreases the diffusion rate because larger molecules move more slowly through membranes.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Compare simple diffusion and facilitated diffusion.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: | Feature | Simple Diffusion | Facilitated Diffusion |
|---|---|---|
| Carrier protein required | No | Yes |
| Saturation possible | No | Yes |
| Specificity | No | Yes |
| Energy required | No | No |
| Example | O2, CO2 | Glucose, amino acids |

| Cell effect | No change | Swelling, possible lysis |

*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate Importance of Feed-forward mechanisms
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: • Anticipates changes in regulated variables
• Improves the speed of the body's homeostatic responses
• Minimizes fluctuations in the level of the variable being regulated
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate 5 examples of negative feedback mechanisms
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: • Osmoregulation
• thermoregulation
• Glucoregulation
• water balance
• pH regulation
• BP regulation
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain the effect of a drop in body temperature on thermoregulatory responses.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: It activates negative feedback: thermoreceptors detect the drop and send signals to the hypothalamus, which activates effectors such as skin (vasoconstriction), muscles (shivering), and piloerection to conserve and generate heat, restoring body temperature.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Describe the effect of feedforward control in anticipation of exercise.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Before exercise starts, brain signals cause an anticipatory increase in heart rate and respiratory rate to meet future oxygen demands, improving efficiency.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain the effect of deviation from a physiological set point.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Deviation from the set point activates the sensor and comparator. The comparator evaluates the magnitude of deviation and signals the effectors to initiate corrective responses. The magnitude of correction is proportional to the degree of deviation.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] What happens when homeostasis is disrupted?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Disruption of homeostasis leads to abnormal regulation of internal variables, resulting in physiological imbalance. If prolonged or severe, it can lead to disease or pathological states.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain the mechanism of negative feedback using thermoregulation.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Cold exposure → thermoreceptors detect ↓ temperature → signals sent to hypothalamus → efferent responses activate vasoconstriction, piloerection, and shivering → heat production and reduced loss → body temperature returns to set point.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain the mechanism of positive feedback in nerve signal generation.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Initial depolarization → voltage-gated Na+ channels open → Na+ influx → further depolarization → more Na+ channels open → continued depolarization until action potential threshold is reached → full nerve signal generated.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Why is positive feedback considered dangerous in most physiological conditions?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Because it amplifies deviations from the set point instead of correcting them, leading to instability.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Why is feedforward control important in homeostatic regulation?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: It anticipates changes before they occur, allowing faster and more efficient physiological responses that reduce the extent of deviation from set points, improving overall stability.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Why is the extracellular fluid (ECF) considered the internal environment of the body?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Because all cells are surrounded by and exchange substances with the ECF, which provides essential ions, oxygen, and nutrients. It ensures that all cells function within a stable, shared environment.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Why is homeostasis considered dynamic rather than fixed?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Because physiological variables are regulated within a range of values, not fixed points. These values fluctuate around a set-point depending on external and internal conditions, allowing flexibility and adaptability.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Compare negative and positive feedback mechanisms.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: | Feature | Negative Feedback | Positive Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| Direction of response | Opposes stimulus | Amplifies stimulus |
| Stability | Maintains stability | Can cause instability |
| Frequency | Common | Rare |
| Outcome | Restores set point | Drives further deviation |
| Example | Thermoregulation | Childbirth |
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Compare negative feedback and feedforward control.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: | Feature | Negative Feedback | Feedforward Control |
|---|---|---|
| Timing | After deviation | Before deviation |
| Trigger | Change detected by sensor | Anticipation of change |
| Purpose | Restore homeostasis | Prevent deviation |
| Type | Reactive | Proactive |
| Example | Blood pressure control | Increased heart rate before exercise |
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Compare set point and deviation in the context of homeostasis.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: | Feature | Set Point | Deviation |
|---|---|---|
| Definition | Optimal target value | Change from the set point |
| Role | Used as reference by comparator | Triggers correction when detected by sensor |
| Example | 37°C (core body temp) | Drop to 35°C triggers thermoregulatory response |
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Match the feedback type to its physiological example: | Feedback Type | Example | |---|---| | A. Negative | 1. Blood pressure regulation | | B. Positive | 2. Uterine contractions | | C. Feedforward | 3. Increased HR before exercise |
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Correct Match: A → 1 B → 2 C → 3
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

Detects change in a physiological variable | | C. Comparator | 3. Compares input to set point and decides action | | D. Effector | 4. Executes response to restore the set point | | E. Set point | 5. Optimal value maintained by feedback |
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Correct Matching: A → 1 B → 2 C → 3 D → 4 E → 5
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the characteristics of active transport.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Occurs against electrochemical gradient
2. Requires energy (usually from ATP)
3. Involves carrier proteins (pumps)
4. Shows saturation kinetics
5. Demonstrates chemical specificity
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate types of primary active transport pumps.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Na+/K+ pump
2. Ca2+ pumps (plasma membrane and SR)
3. H+ pump (in stomach and kidney tubules)
4. Cl- pump
5. Iodine pump (thyroid gland)
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the steps in the Na+-K+ pump cycle.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Binding of 3 Na+ ions inside the cell
2. ATP hydrolysis and phosphorylation of the pump
3. Conformational change releasing Na+ outside
4. Binding of 2 K+ ions from the extracellular fluid
5. Dephosphorylation and return to original conformation with release of K+ inside
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the types of secondary active transport.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Cotransport (symport) - both solutes move in same direction
2. Countertransport (antiport) - solutes move in opposite directions
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the functional roles of the Na+/K+ pump.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Maintains Na+ and K+ gradients across membranes
2. Helps generate resting membrane potential
3. Controls cell volume
4. Provides driving force for secondary active transport
5. Maintains osmotic balance
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Describe the mechanism of primary active transport via the Na+-K+ pump.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Na+ binds to the pump -> ATP is hydrolyzed -> pump is phosphorylated -> conformational change releases Na+ outside -> K+ binds -> pump is dephosphorylated -> returns to original state releasing K+ inside.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain the mechanism of secondary active cotransport.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Na+ is pumped by primary active transport at one side of cell membrane. This creates Na gradient (storing energy) that helps movement of Na into the cell with concentration gradient and a carrier protein couples Na with another substance (e.g., glucose) that transports into or out of the cell against its gradient using the energy stored in Na gradient.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Describe how calcium is removed from the cytoplasm in muscle cells.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Ca2+ is pumped into the sarcoplasmic reticulum using a Ca2+ ATPase pump, which uses ATP to transport Ca2+ against its gradient.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain how antiport (countertransport) works using Na+ and H+.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Na+ enters the cell down its gradient while H+ is expelled against its gradient using an antiport carrier protein; no direct ATP is used, but energy is derived from Na+ gradient.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Why does active transport require energy?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Because it moves substances against their electrochemical gradient, which is an energetically unfavorable process and requires ATP or stored ionic gradients.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Why is the Na+/K+ pump crucial for cell volume regulation?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: It prevents intracellular accumulation of Na+ and associated water entry by osmosis; without it, water would enter the cell causing swelling and rupture.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Why is the Na+ gradient considered a storehouse of energy?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Because it can drive secondary active transport of other molecules against their gradients, such as glucose and amino acids.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Why is the Na+/K+ pump electrogenic?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Because it moves 3 Na+ out and only 2 K+ in, creating a net loss of positive charge from the cell, contributing to membrane potential.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] What is the effect of blocking the Na+/K+ ATPase on the resting membrane potential?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: The resting membrane potential becomes less negative (depolarizes) due to loss of the electrogenic effect of the pump and Na+/K+ gradient dissipation-> loss of excitability.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] What happens to cell volume if the Na+/K+ pump is inhibited?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Intracellular Na+ accumulates, water follows osmotically -> cells swell and may eventually rupture.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] What is the effect of increasing extracellular K+ on Na+/K+ pump activity?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Pump activity increases as extracellular K+ availability facilitates its binding and transport into the cell.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] What is the effect of low Na+ gradient on secondary active transport?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Secondary active transport is impaired since it depends on the energy stored in the Na+ gradient created by the Na+/K+ pump.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Compare primary and secondary active transport.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Feature | Primary Active Transport | Secondary Active Transport
Energy source | Directly from ATP | From ion gradient (usually Na+)
ATP used | Yes | No (indirectly dependent)
Examples | Na+/K+ pump, Ca2+ pump | Na+/glucose symport
Carrier requirement | Yes | Yes
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Compare symport and antiport transport systems.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Feature | Symport (Cotransport) | Antiport (Countertransport)
Direction of movement | Both substances move in same direction | Substances move in opposite directions
Example | Na+ and glucose into cell | Na+ in and H+ out
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Compare facilitated diffusion and active transport.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Feature | Facilitated Diffusion | Active Transport
Energy requirement | No | Yes
Direction of movement | Along concentration gradient | Against concentration gradient
Saturation | Yes | Yes
Carrier protein needed | Yes | Yes
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: A -> 1 B -> 2 C -> 3

*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

**Explanation**: A -> 1 B -> 2 C -> 3

### [QROC] Enumerate the causes of resting membrane potential.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Unequal distribution of ions across the membrane due to:
a. Sodium-potassium pump activity
b. Selective permeability of the cell membrane
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the concentrations of the main ions inside and outside the nerve fiber.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: | Ion | Outside (mEq/L) | Inside (mEq/L) | Ratio (in/out) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Na+ | 145 | 14 | 0.1 |
| K+ | 3-5 | 140 | 35 |
| Cl- | 100 | 5-10 | 10-20 |
| Protein- | 2 gm% | 20 gm% | 10 |
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the roles of Na+/K+ pump in establishing RMP.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Maintains high extracellular Na+ and high intracellular K+
2. Provides net outward movement of positive charge (electrogenic)
3. Prevents excessive intracellular accumulation of Na+
4. Maintains osmotic balance and cell volume
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the steps for measuring RMP using electrodes.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Insert one microelectrode inside the cell
2. Place the second electrode on the external surface
3. Connect both to a galvanometer or oscilloscope
4. Observe the recorded potential difference (~ -70 mV in neurons)
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain how K+ diffusion contributes to RMP.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: • K+ diffuses out of the cell along its concentration gradient
• Inside of the membrane becomes negatively charged due to non-diffusible protein-
• As positive charges build up outside, an opposing electrical gradient forms
• Diffusion stops when electrical and chemical forces balance (~ -90 mV)
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Describe the role of selective membrane permeability in generating RMP.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: • Membrane is highly permeable to K+ but not to Na+ or proteins
• K+ efflux dominates and leads to inside negativity
• Proteins cannot leave -> fixed intracellular negativity
• Na+ influx is limited due to low permeability and active pumping
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Describe how Cl- is prevented from entering the cell despite its gradient.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: • Negatively charged proteins repel Cl-
• Attraction to extracellular Na+ retains Cl- outside
• Although membrane is permeable to Cl-, net movement is minimal
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] What is the effect of inhibiting the Na+/K+ pump on the RMP?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: • Intracellular Na+ increases and K+ decreases
• Cell becomes depolarized (less negative)
• Long-term: cell may swell due to osmotic imbalance
• Electrical gradient collapses
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain the effect of increased extracellular K+ significantly on RMP?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: • K+ gradient decreases
• Less K+ efflux occurs
• RMP becomes less negative (depolarizes)
• May lead to excitability or conduction block
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain the effect of increasing membrane permeability to Na+ at rest?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: • More Na+ enters the cell
• Inside becomes less negative
• RMP depolarizes
• Cell becomes more excitable
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain the effect of failure of the Na+/K+ pump on volume?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: • Na+ accumulates inside
• Water enters osmotically
• Cell swells and may rupture
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Why is the resting membrane potential close to the equilibrium potential of K+? because
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: • The membrane is 50-100 times more permeable to K+ than Na+
• K+ efflux dominates at rest
• Na+ permeability is minimal
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Why are intracellular proteins important in maintaining RMP?
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: • They are negatively charged and are impeded from efflux due to large size -> Contribute to intracellular negativity
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Compare the roles of Na+ and K+ in generating resting membrane potential.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: | Factor | Na+ | K+ |
|---|---|---|
| Concentration Gradient | Higher outside than inside | Higher inside than outside |
| Permeability | Low at rest | High at rest (50-100x Na+) |
| Movement | Limited influx | Major efflux |
| Contribution to RMP | Minor (inward) | Major (outward, sets RMP) |
| Role in Pumping | 3 Na+ out | 2 K+ in |
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

**Explanation**: 1.Increased all properties of the heart (increases heart rate, contractility, excitability and conductivity).
2.Coronary vasodilation → increased coronary blood flow.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate six functions of the greater splanchnic nerve.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Relaxation of the walls and contraction of sphincters of stomach, small intestine, and proximal large intestine.
2. Vasoconstriction of blood vessels in GIT, kidneys, liver, and pancreas.
3. Stimulation of glycogenolysis in liver and lipolysis in adipose tissue.
4. Contraction of splenic capsule releasing RBC-rich blood.
5. Relaxation of gall bladder wall and contraction of sphincter of Oddi.
6. Stimulation of adrenal medulla to secrete catecholamines (adrenaline and noradrenaline).
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

### [QROC] Explain the mechanism of urine retention under sympathetic control.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Sympathetic fibers relax the detrusor muscle (bladder wall) and contract the internal urethral sphincter via hypogastric nerves, preventing urine outflow.

*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

### [QROC] Describe the mechanism of fecal retention by pelvic sympathetic nerves.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Sympathetic fibers inhibit rectal contractions and cause contraction of the internal anal sphincter, promoting fecal storage.

*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Explain the mechanism of ejaculation in males under sympathetic control.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Sympathetic stimulation (T12-L2) mediates emission and ejaculation:
1. Emission: Contracts smooth muscle of vas deferens, seminal vesicles, and prostate, delivering seminal fluid and sperm into prostatic urethra.
2. Bladder neck closure: Contracts internal urethral sphincter to prevent retrograde ejaculation into urinary bladder.
3. Ejaculation: Rhythmic contractions of bulbospongiosus and pelvic floor muscles (via somatic pudendal nerve) forcefully expel semen through penile urethra.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026, Exams, Final 2026*

---

### [QROC] Describe the mechanism of Orbelli phenomenon in sympathetic stimulation.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: • Vasodilation increases oxygen/nutrient supply. • Motor end plate sensitivity to ACh increases. • Phosphorylase enzyme is activated, increasing glycogen breakdown and energy supply to muscles.
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

**Explanation**: Sympathetic fibers innervate arrector pili muscles in the skin. Upon stimulation, these muscles contract and cause hair to stand up.

*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

### [QROC] Enumerate the steps in the synthesis of noradrenaline.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Tyrosine -> Dopa (by Tyrosine hydroxylase)
2. Dopa -> Dopamine (by Dopa decarboxylase)
3. Dopamine -> Noradrenaline (by Dopamine β-hydroxylase)
4. (In adrenal medulla) Noradrenaline -> Adrenaline (by PNMT)
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Enumerate the methods by which noradrenaline is inactivated.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: 1. Active reuptake into nerve endings
2. Diffusion into body fluids and blood
3. Enzymatic degradation:
- Oxidation by monoamine oxidase (MAO)
- Methylation by catechol-O-methyl transferase (COMT)
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

### [QROC] Explain how α2 receptors contribute to feedback inhibition.

**Explanation**: NA binds to presynaptic α2 receptors -> ↓ cAMP -> ↓ calcium influx -> ↓ NA release

*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

### [QROC] Why do sympathetic cholinergic fibers cause vasodilation in skeletal muscle?

*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

### [QROC] Why does adrenaline have stronger metabolic effects than noradrenaline?

### [QROC] Compare α1 and α2 adrenergic receptors.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Feature | α1 Receptors | α2 Receptors
Abundance | More abundant | Less abundant
Location | Postsynaptic membranes of effectors | Presynaptic terminals & some effectors
Action | Mainly excitatory | Mainly inhibitory
Mechanism of action | ↑ intracellular Ca2+ | ↓ cAMP
Example effect | Vasoconstriction, sphincter contraction | Inhibition of NA release
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Compare β1 and β2 receptor actions.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Feature | β1 Receptors | β2 Receptors
Location | Heart, kidney | Bronchi, vessels, GI, urinary bladder
Effect | Excitatory (↑HR, contractility) | Mainly inhibitory (relaxation)
Metabolic Role | Stimulates renin secretion | Stimulates glycogenolysis, insulin release
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Compare the roles of adrenaline and noradrenaline.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Feature | Adrenaline | Noradrenaline
Origin | 80% adrenal medulla, 20% nerve endings | 80% nerve endings, 20% adrenal medulla
Receptor Affinity | Equal α and β (more β2 effect) | Mainly α
Heart Effect | More increase in activity (β1 & β2) | Less effect
Metabolic Effect | Strong (↑ glucose, FA) | Weaker
Bronchodilation | Strong | Weak
BV | Weak VC | Strong VC
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Compare between sympathetic and parasympathetic on salivary glands
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: Feature | Parasympathetic Nervous System | Sympathetic Nervous System
Type of Secretion | Watery (serous), enzyme-poor secretion | Thick (mucous), low-volume secretion
Gland Activity | Strong stimulation of salivary secretion | Mild stimulation of salivary secretion
Primary Neurotransmitter | Acetylcholine (ACh) | Noradrenaline (Norepinephrine)
Receptor Type | Muscarinic (mainly M3) | α and β adrenergic receptors
Effect on Blood Flow | Vasodilation → increases blood flow to glands | Vasoconstriction → decreases blood flow to glands
Overall Effect | Facilitates digestion, promotes oral lubrication | Produces dry mouth in stress (fight or flight

*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

### [QROC] Compare muscarinic vs nicotinic receptor actions of acetylcholine.
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: | Feature | Muscarinic like actions | Nicotinic like actions |
|---|---|---|
| Stimulated by | Muscarine and acetylcholine | Nicotine and acetylcholine |
| Location | - Effector organs supplied by parasympathetic postganglionic fibers<br>- Sweat glands and skeletal muscle BV supplied by Postganglionic sympathetic fibers | - Autonomic ganglia<br>- Adrenal medulla<br>- Neuromuscular junctions |
| Onset & duration of action | Slow onset, prolonged duration | Rapid onset, short duration |
| Main actions | - ↓ Heart rate (bradycardia)<br>- ↑ GIT motility and secretion<br>- Bronchoconstriction<br>- Miosis<br>- Glandular secretion | - Ganglionic transmission<br>- Muscle contraction at neuromuscular junctions |
| Blocked by | Atropine | High dose of nicotine / Ganglion blockers (e.g., hexamethonium) |
| Receptors | M1 to M5 (e.g., M2 in heart, M3 in glands and smooth muscles) | N1 (NMJ) and N2 (autonomic ganglia, adrenal medulla) |
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*

---

### [QROC] Compare between Sympathetic and Parasympathetic Systems
**Correct Answer**: -
**Explanation**: | Sympathetic system | Parasympathetic system |
|---|---|
| Originates in thoracic and lumbar regions of the spinal cord (T1-L3) | Originates in brainstem (cranial nerves III, VII, IX, and X) and sacral region of spinal cord (S2-S4) |
| Ganglia located in paravertebral sympathetic ganglion chain or collateral ganglia | Terminal ganglia located near or embedded within target tissue |
| Short cholinergic preganglionic fibers; long adrenergic postganglionic fiber | Long cholinergic preganglionic fibers; short cholinergic postganglionic fibers |
| Ratio of preganglionic fibers to postganglionic fibers is 1:20 | Ratio of preganglionic fibers to postganglionic fibers is 1:3 |
| Divergence coordinates activity of neurons at multiple levels of spinal cord | Limited divergence |
| Activity often involves mass discharge of entire system | Activity normally to discrete organs |
| Predominates during emergency "fight-or-flight" reactions and exercise | Predominates during quiet resting conditions |
---
*Tags: Year 2026 | Department, Physiology 2026*
